Reference strip · numbers for these chapters The values these ten chapters are graded against: gas exchange, perfusion, and clotting. Scroll it away when you don't need it. ▾
Gas exchange — ABG and oximetry
pH 7.35–7.45
PaCO₂ 35–45 mm Hg — the lung's acid
HCO₃⁻ 21–28 mEq/L — the kidney's base
PaO₂ 80–100 mm Hg
SaO₂ / SpO₂ 95–100%
COPD baseline may be 88–92% — treat the trend, not the textbook
Why
ROME: Respiratory Opposite (pH and CO₂ move opposite ways), Metabolic Equal (pH and HCO₃⁻ move together). Every acid-base item is that one sentence plus asking whether the other system has started to compensate.
Cardiac markers and lipids
Troponin I/T essentially undetectable; rises 3–6 h, peaks ~24 h, stays up 10–14 days
CK-MB rises 4–6 h, peaks 18 h
BNP <100 pg/mL — above that suggests heart failure
Total cholesterol <200 mg/dL
LDL <130 (<100 with CAD) · HDL >45
Triglycerides <150 mg/dL
hsCRP, homocysteine — inflammation and risk markers
Why
Troponin is the answer to "which lab confirms it" because it is specific to cardiac muscle — it is released only when myocardium dies. That is also why it stays elevated for days: the question "is this a new infarct?" is answered by the trend, not a single value.
Hemodynamics and pressures
Cardiac output 4–7 L/min
Cardiac index 2.7–3.2 L/min/m²
Stroke volume 60–130 mL
Ejection fraction 55–70%
MAP 60–70 mm Hg minimum to perfuse organs
Pulse pressure systolic − diastolic (normal ~30–40)
Orthostatic drop ≥20 systolic or ≥10 diastolic
Why
CO = HR × SV, and stroke volume is set by preload, afterload, and contractility. Every cardiac drug and every cardiac problem in these chapters moves one of those four levers — which is why naming the lever tells you the expected effect and the expected side effect.
ECG measurements
Small box 0.04 s · large box 0.20 s
P wave atrial depolarization
PR interval 0.12–0.20 s
QRS 0.06–0.10 s (ventricular depolarization)
QT 0.32–0.44 s (varies with rate)
ST segment isoelectric — elevation or depression is ischemia or injury
6-second strip × 10 = rate for an irregular rhythm
Why
Every rhythm is read with the same five questions: rate, regularity, P for every QRS, PR length, QRS width. The waveform is a map of conduction — a wide QRS means the impulse did not use the normal fast pathway, which is why ventricular beats are wide and supraventricular ones are narrow.
Hematology and clotting
RBC 4.2–5.4 (F) / 4.7–6.1 (M) million/mm³
Hgb 12–16 (F) / 14–18 (M) g/dL
Hct 37–47% (F) / 42–52% (M)
WBC 5,000–10,000/mm³ · Platelets 150,000–400,000
PT 11–12.5 s · INR 0.8–1.1 (therapeutic 2–3)
aPTT 30–40 s (therapeutic 1.5–2.5× control)
D-dimer rises with clot breakdown · ferritin, B₁₂, folate for anemia workup
Why
Two numbers, two drugs: INR follows warfarin, aPTT follows heparin. Both answer the same question — how far from normal clotting have we deliberately moved this patient — which is why the nursing action for a high value is always bleeding precautions plus the antidote (vitamin K for warfarin, protamine sulfate for heparin).
Exam 2 prep · Med-surg/Ch 22 · 23 · 24 · 25 · 27 · 28 · 29 · 30 · 33 · 34/Info page

Oxygen in,
blood around.

Ten chapters, but only two questions: is the patient moving air, and is the blood reaching tissue. Respiratory chapters are the first; cardiac and vascular chapters are the second; hematology is the carrier and the clot. Every fact below sits inside an ADPIE step, carries a rationale, and is traced down to the anatomy and physiology that makes it true.

00 · Start here

The two questions these chapters answer

1 · Gas exchange

Chapters 22 through 25. Air has to move in (ventilation), cross the membrane (diffusion), and find hemoglobin to ride on. Anything that blocks the airway, stiffens the lung, or floods the alveolus breaks one of those three steps — and the assessment finding tells you which.

2 · Perfusion

Chapters 27 through 30, plus 33 and 34. A pump (rate, rhythm, squeeze), a pipe (open, elastic, intact), and a carrier (red cells, hemoglobin, platelets). Every condition in these chapters is one of those three failing, and the cues follow the failure backward and forward from the heart.

How to read a chapter here

Intro → the Connection Map (the big picture) → the anatomy and physiology underneath → a scenario → A, D, P, I, E → the supporting detail cards. Read one chapter in that order in a sitting, cover each Why and say it in your own words, then drill that chapter on the recall page the same day. Hover any a word taken apart into its rootsdotted underlinedotted term to see the word taken apart.

22 · Chapter 22
Gas exchange · perfusion

Assessment of the respiratory system

On Exam 2 · 5 questionsHealth promotion & maintenance·Calculating pack-years·Age-related respiratory changes

Intro. This chapter is the toolkit every later respiratory chapter uses. You are answering one question in four ways — inspection, palpation, percussion, auscultation — and the question is whether air is reaching alveoli that can actually exchange it. Learn what each finding means mechanically and you will never memorize a list of adventitious sounds again.

Connection Map How this chapter fits together
1 · Normal physiologyAir moves in (ventilation), crosses a one-cell membrane (diffusion), and binds hemoglobin; CO₂ leaves the same way.
2 · What failsAny step can fail: blocked airway, stiff or flooded alveoli, weak muscles, or depressed drive.
3 · What the nurse seesTachypnea, accessory muscles, tripoding, restlessness, adventitious sounds, falling SpO₂.
4 · What proves itSpO₂ (oxygenation), ETCO₂ and ABG PaCO₂ (ventilation), CXR, PFTs, bronchoscopy, thoracentesis.
5 · What targets itOxygen, upright position, airway clearance — and fixing whichever step failed.
6 · If it worsensFatigue → hypoventilation → rising CO₂ → acidosis → respiratory failure.
7 · Emergency triggerSilent chest, stridor, new confusion, SpO₂ <90%, RR falling with drowsiness, tracheal deviation.
Cause and effect
Opioid or sedative→respiratory center depressed→slow, shallow breathing→CO₂ rises while SpO₂ looks fine on oxygen→monitor RR, sedation and ETCO₂, not SpO₂ alone
Fluid in alveoli→fine crackles that do not clear with cough→longer diffusion path→SpO₂ falls→oxygen plus treating the fluid cause
Rhonchi vs fine crackles
Rhonchi = secretions in big airways, often clear with cough. Fine crackles = alveoli popping open against fluid, do not clear.
Dead space vs shunt
Dead space (PE) = air without blood. Shunt (pneumonia) = blood without air — responds poorly to oxygen alone.
Why the drug makes sense
Amiodarone/bleomycin → lung injury; ACE inhibitors → bradykinin → dry cough; nonselective beta blockers → beta2 blocked → bronchospasm. Drug cards →
Connections to other chapters
Every later chapter uses this toolkit: ch 24 obstruction, ch 25 consolidation, ch 29 pulmonary edema. Anemia (ch 34) lowers oxygen content even with normal SpO₂.
Monitor
Stable SpO₂ at the client’s baseline, clear speech, unchanged sounds.
Act now
New confusion, stridor, silent chest, sudden dyspnea after a procedure, SpO₂ <90% not responding.
Down to the anatomy and physiology
Upper airway — nose and sinuses (warm, humidify, filter), pharynx, larynx with the epiglottis and vocal cords. This is where obstruction is mechanical and fast, and where the the flap that closes the airway when you swallowepi- upon · glottis the opening of the larynxepi·glottis protects the lungs from everything you swallow.
Lower airway — trachea, right and left mainstem bronchi (the right is more vertical, which is why aspiration and a dislodged tube go right), bronchioles with smooth muscle that constricts in asthma, and alveoli lined with the fatty film that keeps alveoli from collapsingsurface-active agentsurfactant.
Alveolar-capillary membrane — one cell thick on each side. gases moving from where there is more to where there is lessdia- apart · -fus- to pourDiffusion happens down a pressure gradient, so anything that thickens the membrane (fluid, fibrosis, pus) or removes surface area (collapse, resection, emphysema) reduces it.
Ventilation versus perfusion (V/Q) — air without blood is dead space (pulmonary embolism); blood without air is a shunt (pneumonia, alveoli that never fully inflateatel- incomplete · -ectasis expansionatel·ectasis, pulmonary edema). Both cause hypoxemia; only one improves much with oxygen alone.
Control of breathing — central chemoreceptors respond to CO₂ and pH (the normal drive); peripheral chemoreceptors respond to low O₂. This is the physiology behind the caution about high-flow oxygen in chronic CO₂ retainers.
On examAging changes — alveoli lose elasticity, the chest wall stiffens, respiratory muscle strength falls, cough and cilia weaken, and PaO₂ falls with age. Same pneumonia, fewer symptoms, less reserve.
Why
Every respiratory finding you will be asked about is one of four mechanical problems: the airway is narrowed, the alveoli are collapsed, the membrane is thickened, or the blood is not arriving. Wheeze = narrowed. Crackles = fluid or reopening alveoli. Diminished = air not getting there. Absent with tracheal deviation = a space filled by air or blood pushing the lung away. Naming the mechanism gives you both the priority and the intervention.
On exam
Inhalation injury and smoking cessation
The single biggest modifiable factor — exposure to inhalation irritants, above all cigarette smoke, is the most common cause of chronic respiratory problems and physical limitation. The damaging compounds are tar, nicotine, and carbon monoxide.
Quantify it — pack-years = packs per day × years smoked. Ask separately about secondhand exposure (same environment) and thirdhand (smoke clinging to hair, clothing, and surfaces).
Catch the social smoker — ask "do you ever smoke in social situations?" They do not identify as smokers, yet intermittent smoking still damages and still leads to addiction.
Gauge dependence — do you smoke as soon as you wake? wake at night to smoke? find it hard not to smoke where it is prohibited? smoke when ill? More yes answers, greater dependence.
The 5 As — Ask about use, Advise to quit, Assess willingness, Assist with the attempt, Arrange follow-up. It is a brief intervention, under three minutes, and it is a nursing responsibility at every encounter.
Nicotine replacement — patches, gum, and lozenges over the counter; nasal spray and inhaler by prescription. Never smoke while using NRT — the combined nicotine load raises the risk of stroke and heart attack.
Drugs — bupropion reduces cravings, withdrawal, and associated depression but carries a black box warning for suicidal thoughts and behavior: teach the patient and family to report any change in thinking or behavior immediately. Varenicline blocks nicotine receptors so smoking is less rewarding.
Vaping and hookah — hookah exposure is as great as or greater than cigarettes despite the widespread belief it is safer. EVALI (e-cigarette or vaping product use–associated lung injury) has caused serious injury in people vaping under two years, with higher risk from THC-containing products.
Occupational and environmental screening — I PREPARE — Investigate exposures, Present work, Residence, Environment, Past work, Activities and hobbies, Resources and referrals, Educate. Higher-risk trades include coal miners, stone masons, welders, woodworkers, potters, bakers, printers, and grain and foundry workers.
Why
Exposure history is a physical assessment finding, not social small talk — it changes the differential before you touch the patient. And the reason the 5 As are compressed into three minutes is that willingness to quit is not a precondition for the conversation: advice given briefly and repeatedly at every contact is what moves patients between stages, which is why "the patient isn't interested" is not a reason to skip it.
Reading the numbers — oximetry, capnography, PFTs
Oximetry thresholds — normal 95–100%. Below 90% in an adult without chronic lung disease demands immediate assessment and intervention; below 85% tissues struggle to oxygenate; below 75% is critical and life-threatening. In chronic hypercarbia, above 90% may be the accepted target.
When the reading lies — poor perfusion, hypothermia, motion, dark nail polish, and carbon monoxide poisoning (falsely normal). With impaired peripheral perfusion the forehead is the most accurate site.
Health equity — oximetry is less accurate in patients with darker skin and hypoxia is missed more often, so judge the trend alongside ABGs and clinical signs rather than one absolute number.
Capnometry versus capnography — both measure exhaled CO₂; capnometry gives a color or number, capnography adds the waveform. Normal end-tidal CO₂ is 35–45 mm Hg.
Why ETCO₂ warns earlier — carbon dioxide leaves the body more readily than oxygen enters it, so ETCO₂ shifts before hypoxia is detectable. Raised by hypoventilation, partial obstruction, rebreathing, fever, acidosis; lowered by pulmonary embolism, apnea, total obstruction, tube malposition, and cardiac arrest. During a code it also reports whether CPR is effective and whether circulation has returned.
PFTs — what and why — most often ordered to find the cause of dyspnea, and with exercise they separate lung causes from cardiac ones and from muscle weakness. Results are compared with expected values for age, sex, race, height, weight, and smoking status.
PFT preparation — no smoking for 6–8 hours; bronchodilators may be withheld 4–6 hours depending on the question being asked. The test is effort-dependent, so coaching quality is data quality.
The values — FVC, all air forcibly exhaled after a maximal breath in. FEV1, the volume in the first second. The FEV1/FVC ratio is the hallmark of obstruction — reduced in obstructive disease, normal or high in restrictive. RV, FRC, and TLC rise with air trapping in emphysema and fall in restrictive disease. DLCO measures the alveolocapillary surface area and drops in emphysema, fibrosis, and pulmonary hypertension.
After the test — assess for bronchospasm or increased dyspnea from the repeated maneuvers and document any drugs given during testing.
Why
Saturation and ventilation are different questions, and the pulse oximeter only answers the first. A patient on high-flow oxygen can hold a perfect SpO₂ while their CO₂ climbs and they slide into narcosis — which is exactly the gap capnography fills, and exactly why an opioid-sedated patient is monitored for respiratory rate and CO₂, not saturation alone.
Oxygen as a drug — devices, doses, hazards
The principle — oxygen is prescribed with a dose, has therapeutic and toxic effects, and requires assessment of response. The goal is the lowest FiO₂ that achieves an acceptable blood oxygen level. Room air is 21%; most hypoxic patients need 2–4 L/min by cannula or up to 40% by Venturi mask. Fever, sepsis, and anemia raise the requirement.
Low-flow versus high-flow — low-flow systems do not supply the whole inspired volume, so room air dilutes the oxygen and the delivered concentration varies with the patient's rate and pattern. High-flow systems supply the total inspired volume and deliver a precise concentration.
Nasal cannula — 1–6 L/min, roughly 24% at 1 L rising about 4% per liter to 44%. Not run above 6 L/min: anatomic dead space is already full, so exchange does not improve and the mucosa dries and cracks.
Simple mask — 40–60% at 5–8 L/min, with a minimum of 5 L/min to flush exhaled CO₂ out of the mask. Partial rebreather — 60–75% at 6–11 L/min; the reservoir bag must stay slightly inflated at end-inspiration and must not kink.
Nonrebreather — the highest low-flow concentration, 80–95%+ at 10–15 L/min. Check at least hourly that the valve and flaps move freely; a stuck valve drops the FiO₂ sharply and can suffocate the patient.
Venturi mask — the most accurate concentration short of intubation, 24–50%. Never cover or obstruct the entrainment orifice. High-flow nasal cannula — precise FiO₂ at 30–60 L/min, heated and humidified, better tolerated, and the patient can still talk and clear secretions. Face tent for facial trauma or burns; aerosol mask when high humidity is needed.
NPPV — positive pressure without intubation for dyspnea, hypercarbia, COPD exacerbation, pulmonary edema, and acute asthma. CPAP holds one pressure through the whole cycle and reopens collapsed alveoli; BiPAP delivers a higher inspiratory and lower expiratory pressure, improving tidal volume.
Combustion — oxygen does not burn or explode but it enhances combustion: no open flame or smoking, signage posted, grounded three-prong equipment, no frayed cords, and no high-alcohol or oil-based solutions in the room.
Oxygen toxicity — determined by concentration, duration, and existing lung disease; above 50% for more than 24–48 hours may injure the lung. Early signs: dyspnea, nonproductive cough, substernal chest pain, GI upset, crackles. Notify when the PaO₂ rises above 90 mm Hg so the dose can come down — prevention is the only management.
Absorptive atelectasis — nitrogen (79% of room air) normally splints alveoli open. High oxygen washes the nitrogen out, oxygen diffuses into the blood, and the alveolus collapses. Watch for new crackles and reduced breath sounds; assess every 1–2 hours when high-level therapy begins.
Drying and infection — humidify above 4 L/min and confirm the oxygen actually bubbles through the water. Drain condensation by disconnecting the tubing; never drain it back into the humidifier. Check ears, neck, and face every 4–8 hours, mouth care every 8 hours, and lubricate nostrils and lips with a nonpetroleum product.
Home oxygen — Medicare criteria are a PaO₂ below 55 mm Hg or SpO₂ below 88% on room air at rest. Sources: compressed gas in a green tank (check the gauge, secure the tank in a stand), liquid oxygen (lasts longer, costs more), and a concentrator that extracts nitrogen from room air — which needs power, so teach a backup plan for outages.
Why
Every hazard on this list comes from the same fact: oxygen is a treatment with a dose-dependent toxicity, so "more is safer" is wrong in three separate ways. Too much for too long inflames the lung, washes out the nitrogen that holds alveoli open, and in the chronic retainer blunts the very stimulus to breathe. That is why the order names a dose and why titrating down when the PaO₂ climbs is a nursing action rather than an afterthought.
Endoscopy, thoracentesis, and biopsy — the nursing care
The three endoscopic studies — bronchoscopy views the airways to the secondary bronchi and takes samples; laryngoscopy assesses vocal cords, removes foreign bodies, and biopsies; mediastinoscopy enters through the chest wall above the sternum to examine between the lungs.
Before — consent, two patient identifiers per National Patient Safety Goals, NPO 4–8 hours, and often CBC, platelets, prothrombin time, electrolytes, and a chest x-ray.
After bronchoscopy — NPO until the gag reflex returns; vital signs, breath sounds, and oxygen saturation every 15 minutes for 2 hours. Blood-streaked sputum is expected; frank bleeding, stridor, or new dyspnea is reported.
Thoracentesis positioning — sitting upright leaning over a bedside table, arms supported; the patient is told to hold still and not cough or breathe deeply during needle insertion. The nurse steadies the patient and the table and watches for shock, pain, nausea, pallor or ash-gray skin, diaphoresis, cyanosis, tachypnea, and dyspnea.
Thoracentesis limits and after care — generally no more than about 1000 mL at once to avoid re-expansion pulmonary edema; chest x-ray afterward; document the patient's response, the volume and character of the fluid, specimens sent, the puncture site, and respiratory assessment before, during, and after.
Teach the pneumothorax signs for the next 24 hours — pain on the affected side worst at the end of inhalation and exhalation, rapid heart rate, rapid shallow breathing, air hunger, a prominent side that does not move with breathing, the trachea slanting toward the unaffected side, a new nagging cough, and cyanosis: go to the emergency department immediately.
Lung biopsy — transbronchial or needle aspiration during bronchoscopy, percutaneous transthoracic aspiration, or an open biopsy by thoracotomy with a chest tube. A chest x-ray or CT always follows to rule out pneumothorax. Vital signs and breath sounds at least every 4 hours for 24 hours; report reduced or absent breath sounds immediately, and watch for hemoptysis.
Why
Two nursing points cover every procedure in this list, which is why they are worth holding as a pair rather than as seven separate protocols: protect the airway until the gag reflex returns, and watch the pleural space for a pneumothorax. Anything entering the chest can let air in where it does not belong, and anything numbing the throat can let food in where it does not belong.
Breath sounds — sound to mechanism
Fine crackles — alveoli popping open against fluid or collapse. Late inspiratory, at the bases. Heart failure, pneumonia, atelectasis, fibrosis. Do not clear with cough.
Coarse crackles — air bubbling through secretions in larger airways. Wetter, louder, may partially clear.
Wheeze — air forced through narrowed airways. Expiratory in asthma and COPD; a silent chest in a known wheezer is worse, not better, because no air is moving at all.
Rhonchi — low-pitched snoring from secretions in the large airways; often clears with an effective cough.
Stridor — high-pitched crowing on inspiration from upper airway narrowing. Emergency. Airway, call for help, prepare for intubation.
Pleural friction rub — creaking leather, inspiration and expiration, painful, unchanged by coughing. Inflamed pleura.
Diminished or absent — air is not reaching that area: effusion, pneumothorax, consolidation, or a very poor effort.
Why
Sounds are made by air meeting resistance, so the sound names the obstacle: fluid pops, narrowing whistles, secretions rattle, inflamed surfaces grate, and nothing at all means nothing is moving. The exam version of this is always "which finding requires immediate action" — and the answer is the one that means the airway itself is closing: stridor, or a wheeze that went silent.
Diagnostic tests, and your job around each
Pulse oximetry — noninvasive saturation. Unreliable with poor perfusion, hypothermia, motion, dark nail polish, and falsely normal in carbon monoxide poisoning.
ABG — the truth about ventilation and acid-base. Apply pressure 5 minutes after an arterial draw (20 minutes on anticoagulants), place the sample on ice, and note the oxygen the patient was on.
Chest x-ray — structure, infiltrates, effusion, pneumothorax, tube and line placement.
Pulmonary function tests — volumes and flow rates. No bronchodilator or smoking for 4–6 hours before; the test is effort-dependent, so coaching matters.
a scope passed into the bronchi to look and samplebronch/o- airway · -scopy to lookBroncho·scopy — consent, NPO 4–8 hours before, sedation. After: NPO until the gag reflex returns, watch for bleeding, and expect blood-streaked sputum; report frank bleeding, stridor, or dyspnea.
a needle draining fluid from the pleural spacethorac/o- chest · -centesis puncture to withdrawThoraco·centesis — consent; position sitting up leaning over a table; hold still, do not cough during insertion. After: chest x-ray, watch for pneumothorax (sudden dyspnea, tracheal deviation, absent sounds), and limit removal to about 1000 mL at a time.
Sputum culture — early morning, deep cough not saliva, rinse the mouth with water first, and collect before antibiotics.
Why
Every airway procedure shares two nursing points: the gag reflex and the pleural space. Sedation and topical anesthesia remove the reflex that keeps food out of the lung, so NPO until it returns is not a formality. And any needle or scope near the pleura can let air in, which is why a new sudden dyspnea after a thoracentesis is a pneumothorax until proven otherwise.
On exam
Questions to ask this patient
"How far can you walk before you have to stop — and has that changed?"
"How many pillows do you sleep on? Do you wake up short of breath?"
"Tell me about the cough. Is anything coming up? What color, how much?"
"Did you ever smoke? How many packs a day, and for how many years?" — then compute pack-years.
"What do you do, or did you do, for work? Any dust, chemicals, asbestos, or fumes?"
"Which inhalers do you have at home? Show me how you use them."
"What makes it worse — cold air, activity, lying down, mornings?"
Why
Dyspnea has no lab value — the only measure is what the patient can no longer do, which is why the useful question is about blocks and stairs rather than "are you short of breath." "Show me how you use it" is also the whole inhaler item: most patients who are "adherent" are using the device wrong, and demonstration is the only assessment that detects it.
The nursing process for this chapter

Respiratory assessment always answers the same question in the same order: is the airway open, is air moving, and is it reaching alveoli that can exchange it. The process below applies to any patient with a breathing problem, whatever the diagnosis turns out to be.

A — Assess
On exam
History
Smoking in pack-years, occupational and environmental exposure using I PREPARE, allergies, childhood and family lung disease, travel and geographic history, immunizations, and current drugs — including the ones that damage lungs (bleomycin, amiodarone) and the ones that cause cough (ACE inhibitors, ARBs). Then the symptom story: cough and its timing, sputum volume in teaspoons or cups and its color, hemoptysis, chest pain, and dyspnea graded by what the patient can no longer do — blocks walked, stairs climbed, pillows needed.
Inspect
Position and work of breathing first — tripod posture, pursed lips, accessory muscle use, retractions, seesaw motion beat the pulse oximeter as a first assessment. Then rate, depth, and rhythm; barrel chest (AP:lateral approaching 1:1); clubbing (nail angle 160° → 180°); cyanosis at lips, oral mucosa, and conjunctivae; and long-term signs — weight loss, thin limbs, hypertrophied neck and chest muscles.
Palpate and percuss
Fremitus (increased over consolidation, decreased over fluid or air), crepitus, tracheal position — pushed away by tension pneumothorax or effusion, pulled toward atelectasis or resection — chest expansion symmetry, and percussion notes: resonant, hyperresonant (trapped air), dull (fluid or solid), flat, tympanic.
Auscultate and measure
Apex to base, side to side, comparing left with right, mouth open, stethoscope on skin. Name each adventitious sound by its mechanism. Then SpO₂ read as a trend, ETCO₂ where available, ABGs by ROME, and the results of imaging, PFTs, and any procedure already done.
Why
The order is not arbitrary — it is triage. Observation identifies the patient in trouble before any equipment does, which matters because the two findings that demand immediate action, stridor and a wheeze gone silent, are both heard rather than measured, and a pulse oximeter can read normal while an airway is closing.
D — Diagnose & prioritize
The pattern to match: work of breathing + adventitious sounds + saturation + the mechanism they point to (narrowed airway, collapsed alveoli, thickened membrane, or blood not arriving).
1. Decreased gas exchange related to ventilation/perfusion imbalance or alveolar-capillary membrane changes — evidenced by hypoxemia, abnormal ABGs, and confusion or restlessness.
2. Ineffective airway clearance related to thick secretions, weak cough, or bronchospasm — evidenced by rhonchi, coarse crackles, and ineffective cough effort.
3. Ineffective breathing pattern related to increased work of breathing — evidenced by tachypnea, accessory muscle use, and retractions.
4. Activity intolerance related to reduced oxygen delivery.
5. Anxiety related to dyspnea and the sensation of suffocation.
6. Need for health teaching related to smoking cessation, inhaler technique, oxygen safety, and procedure aftercare.
Why
Airway clearance outranks gas exchange whenever secretions or obstruction are the cause, because clearing the airway is the gas-exchange intervention. And restlessness is ranked as hypoxia, not anxiety — sedating the restless respiratory patient treats your discomfort rather than theirs.
P — Plan
Airway remains patent; no stridor, and no silent chest in a patient who was wheezing.
Saturation at or above the ordered target — commonly 95%, or 88–92% in chronic carbon dioxide retention — on the lowest effective FiO₂.
ABGs move toward normal ranges; ETCO₂ stays 35–45 mm Hg where monitored.
Secretions cleared with an effective cough; adventitious sounds diminish and breath sounds are audible in all fields.
Completes ADLs with rest periods and no dyspnea at rest.
Patient states inhaler technique, oxygen safety rules, and the symptoms that mean returning for care — and after any airway procedure, states the 24-hour pneumothorax warning signs.
Why
The oxygen outcome is written as the lowest effective FiO₂, not the highest saturation, because oxygen has a dose-dependent toxicity. Writing “SpO₂ 100%” as a goal invites absorptive atelectasis, lung injury, and in the retainer a blunted drive to breathe.
I — Implement
Independent
Position upright or high-Fowler to maximize chest expansion. Coughing and deep breathing with an incentive spirometer on a schedule; reposition regularly. Hydration to thin secretions. Pace activity and cluster care. Humidify oxygen above 4 L/min, drain condensation away from the humidifier, and protect skin at the ears, neck, and nose. Post oxygen signage and remove fire hazards. Teach cessation with the 5 As at every encounter. Stay with the patient in acute distress.
Dependent
Oxygen titrated to the ordered target by the correct device — and check the nonrebreather valve and flaps hourly, never occlude the Venturi entrainment port. Bronchodilators, steroids, mucolytics, antibiotics as ordered. ABGs, cultures, imaging, PFTs. NPPV (CPAP to reopen alveoli, BiPAP to improve tidal volume) and preparation for intubation when indicated.
Procedure care
NPO until the gag reflex returns after any throat-anesthetized procedure. After bronchoscopy, vitals, breath sounds, and saturation every 15 minutes for 2 hours. Position and coach for thoracentesis, then chest x-ray and pneumothorax surveillance. After lung biopsy, assessment at least every 4 hours for 24 hours — report reduced or absent breath sounds immediately.
Collaborative
Respiratory therapy for devices and technique; pulmonary rehabilitation; dietitian for the weight loss of chronic lung disease; smoking cessation and home oxygen referrals, including a power-outage backup plan for a concentrator.
Why
Most of the independent column is mechanical rather than pharmacologic — position, humidity, hydration, spirometry — because most of what goes wrong in this chapter is mechanical: alveoli collapse, secretions dry into plugs, and the chest cannot expand. Those are nursing problems with nursing solutions.
E — Evaluate
The cues that named the problem are the cues that measure the fix: work of breathing settles, accessory muscle use and retractions resolve, adventitious sounds diminish, saturation holds at target on less oxygen, and ABGs normalize. The patient completes a sentence without stopping for breath and walks further before stopping. Teach-back confirms inhaler technique, oxygen safety, and when to return.
Unmet looks like: stridor, a wheeze that has gone silent, a rising ETCO₂ with a normal saturation, new tracheal deviation with absent breath sounds, or a patient becoming restless and then quiet. Each escalates rather than being re-planned.
Why
The most dangerous evaluation error here is reading less noise as improvement. A wheezing chest that falls silent and a restless patient who becomes calm are both usually deterioration — the first because no air is moving, the second because hypoxia has progressed to obtundation.
Chapter 22 check Test this chapter now — questions first, answers and rationales after. Recall questions NCLEX set Missed concepts
23 · Chapter 23
Gas exchange · tissue integrity

Noninfectious upper respiratory conditions

Not on the study guideNo questions listed for this chapter — lowest priority.

Intro. Everything in this chapter happens above the trachea, which means every problem is a plumbing problem with a very short fuse: the upper airway is the only part of the respiratory system where swelling, blood, or a foreign body can close the whole thing in minutes. The nursing question is never "how bad is this" but "is the airway open, and how fast is that changing."

Connection Map How this chapter fits together
1 · Normal physiologyThe upper airway is one lumen with no backup; it warms, filters and humidifies air.
2 · What failsObstruction (edema, collapse, bleeding, tumor, trauma) narrows that single tube.
3 · What the nurse seesStridor, drooling, snoring/apnea, hoarseness, epistaxis, clear nasal drainage after trauma.
4 · What proves itObservation of airway sounds, sleep study (OSA), glucose/halo test (CSF), imaging for fractures.
5 · What targets itCPAP splints collapse; pressure and leaning forward stop anterior bleeding; tracheostomy bypasses obstruction.
6 · If it worsensComplete obstruction, aspiration, hypoxic arrest.
7 · Emergency triggerStridor, inability to swallow secretions, dislodged fresh tracheostomy, flap color change, packing in the throat.
Cause and effect
Post-op neck swelling→lumen narrows→airflow falls with the square of the radius→stridor and drooling→stay, call for help, prepare emergency airway
Tracheostomy vs total laryngectomy
Trach: upper airway still connected. Laryngectomy: permanent stoma, no mouth-to-lung path — oxygen goes to the stoma.
Why the drug makes sense
Oxymetazoline → alpha vasoconstriction → shrinks mucosa and slows bleeding; prolonged use → vessels rebound → worse congestion. Opioids → less drive + more airway collapse in OSA. Drug cards →
Connections to other chapters
Ch 22 stridor recognition; ch 30 anticoagulants and ch 34 low platelets worsen epistaxis.
Monitor
Controlled bleeding, CPAP adherence, stable trach with spare tube at bedside.
Act now
Stridor, drooling, trach dislodged, pale/cool or dusky flap, CSF leak.
Down to the anatomy
The nose is vascular on purpose — it warms and humidifies air — and Kiesselbach plexus in the anterior septum is why most nosebleeds are anterior and reachable. Posterior bleeds come from larger vessels, bleed into the throat, and are far more dangerous.
The pharynx collapses during sleep when muscle tone falls. Anything that narrows it — obesity, large tonsils, a small jaw, alcohol, sedatives — turns that into repeated pauses in breathing during sleepa- without · -pnea breathinga·pnea.
The larynx holds the vocal cords and the epiglottis. Remove it and the patient breathes through a permanent neck stoma with no connection between nose and lungs: no humidification, no filtering, no smell, and no voice through the mouth.
Trauma and swelling anywhere in this space reduces the lumen by the square of the radius — halving the diameter quarters the airflow. That is why upper airway problems deteriorate so suddenly rather than gradually.
Why
Upper airway = one lumen, no redundancy. The lower airway has millions of parallel paths, so losing some is survivable; the upper airway has one, so losing it is immediate. Every priority answer in this chapter follows from that single fact.
Obstructive sleep apnea — the full picture
The mechanism — during sleep the head and neck muscles relax and the tongue and soft palate displace backward. Neural control of chest movement stays intact, so the effort continues against a closed airway. Apnea raises CO₂ and drops pH, the chemoreceptors fire, the patient partially wakes and corrects the obstruction — sometimes as often as every 5 minutes, which prevents the deep sleep needed for restoration.
Definitions — OSA is partial or complete airway collapse during sleep; OSAS is symptomatic OSA, with apneic episodes of 10 seconds or longer and intermittent hypoxia.
Underdiagnosis — an estimated 90% of adults with OSA are undiagnosed, partly because heavy snoring is dismissed as normal. Risk rises with age, male sex, obesity, and enlarged tonsils, uvula, or adenoids, and increases sharply in both sexes after 60.
Health equity — Black adults, especially Black men, have a higher incidence and more severe disease. Three drivers: reduced access and screening, sleepiness attributed to other conditions, and mistrust or misunderstanding of the risk. Sleep disorders are underdiagnosed in specific populations, so screen deliberately rather than waiting for a complaint.
What to ask — daytime sleepiness or falling asleep reading, at a computer, or driving; being woken by their own snoring; frequency of nightmares; whether a family member has witnessed the breathing pattern — breaths spacing out, a pause, then gasping and snorting with partial waking. Ask about over-the-counter sleep aids and evening alcohol, both of which worsen it.
GERD connection — many patients develop nighttime reflux from the strong abdominal and chest effort during apneas. Ask about waking with heartburn, stomach contents in the mouth, or a burning choking cough.
What to examine — height and weight, the jaw, external neck, and chin (OSA goes with a retracted lower jaw, smaller chin, shorter neck), and the oral cavity for pharynx shape, uvula size, tongue thickness, and enlarged tonsils or soft palate. Check blood pressure and rhythm: hypertension that does not respond as expected to therapy is a classic clue.
Psychosocial — irritability, depression, loss of interest in social activity, poor recall and concentration. Long-term untreated OSA raises the risk of mild cognitive impairment and dementia.
Diagnosis — a screening questionnaire first (STOP-Bang, Epworth Sleepiness Scale, Pittsburgh Sleep Quality Index, Multiple Sleep Latency Test), then possibly a home study, then the definitive polysomnography: an overnight laboratory study recording ECG, EEG, oximetry, eye movement (EOM), and muscle activity (EMG) with direct observation of sleep depth, respiratory effort, saturation, CO₂, and movement.
Nonsurgical management — weight loss and position change may correct mild disease. Oral appliances (maxillomandibular advancement) hold the jaw forward, useful for a retracted jaw but bulky and a risk to oral mucosal integrity. CPAP is the mainstay: one continuous pressure through inhalation and exhalation via mask or nasal pillows, requiring a proper tight seal. Drug therapy has not been successful — sedatives worsen OSA and stimulants do not treat the cause.
CPAP adherence and care — modern machines are quiet and humidified; Medicare covers much of the cost for older adults who use it at least 6 hours daily. Clean the mask and tubing daily, use distilled water, never share equipment — with humidification, fungal infection is possible.
Surgical options — reserved for anatomic causes and severe resistant disease. An implanted hypoglossal nerve stimulator (electrode in the neck, generator in the chest) contracts the airway muscles without waking the patient. Tonsillectomy, adenoidectomy, uvulectomy, or septal repair address specific anatomy. UPPP remodels the posterior oropharynx; older versions removed extensive tissue and risked permanent taste and voice changes, while modUPPP repositions and reinforces support structures instead, with 2+ days in hospital and 3–6 weeks of recovery. Tracheostomy is a last resort.
After surgery — assess every 2 hours for the first 24 hours for airway narrowing: rising respiratory effort, stridor, drooling or inability to swallow secretions, a smaller oropharynx, falling saturation, or rising ETCO₂ — and call the Rapid Response Team. Give analgesia on a schedule, not PRN, because the oropharynx is richly innervated; avoid aspirin and NSAIDs for the first 24 hours. Darker blood mixed with secretions is normal; bright red or increasing bleeding is reported. Watch for excessive swallowing or belching.
Why
Opioids are the recurring danger in this population, and the mechanism explains why: OSA exists because muscle tone fails to hold the airway open during sleep, and opioids and sedatives remove exactly that tone. So the drug given for postoperative pain reproduces the disease — which is why these patients are monitored continuously, dosed cautiously, and why sedatives are never the answer to their insomnia.
Obstruction, epistaxis, and trauma
Causes of upper airway obstruction — tongue edema (surgery, trauma, angioedema), tongue occlusion (lost gag reflex, lost tone, unconsciousness), laryngeal edema (smoke or toxin inhalation, inflammation, anaphylaxis), peritonsillar or pharyngeal abscess, head and neck cancer, thick secretions, stroke and cerebral edema, facial or laryngeal trauma or burns, and foreign-body aspiration.
The preventable one — thickly crusted oral and nasopharyngeal secretions can completely block the airway and cause death. It comes from poor oral hygiene, and it is eliminated by nursing care. Highest risk: altered mental status, dehydration, inability to communicate, ineffective cough, aspiration risk. Assess oral care needs daily and make sure assistive personnel understand why the technique matters.
Recognizing it — partial obstruction gives vague general signs: diaphoresis, tachycardia, anxiety, raised blood pressure. Then hypoxia and hypercarbia, restlessness, increasing anxiety, sternal retractions, "seesawing" chest motion, abdominal movement, and a feeling of impending doom. Monitor with oximetry and ETCO₂ and watch continuously for stridor, cyanosis, and falling consciousness.
Acting on it — extend the head and neck slightly and insert a nasal or oral airway if the tongue or secretions are the cause; suction. For a foreign body, abdominal thrusts in the conscious patient. If the patient is unconscious, start CPR chest compressions instead of thrusts, even when a foreign body is suspected. Beyond that: laryngoscopy, endotracheal intubation (placed before swelling makes it impossible), cricothyroidotomy through the cricothyroid membrane, or tracheotomy — which takes 5–10 minutes electively but can establish an airway in under 2 minutes as an emergency.
Epistaxis — why it bleeds — the nose is dense with capillaries. Causes include trauma, hypertension, blood dyscrasias such as leukemia, inflammation, tumor, low humidity, nose blowing and picking, chronic cocaine use, and NG suctioning. Older adults tend to bleed posteriorly.
Emergency care — Standard Precautions; sit the patient upright and leaning forward to keep blood out of the larynx; reassure and keep them quiet, since anxiety raises blood pressure; apply direct lateral pressure for 15–20 minutes with ice or cool compresses; pack both nares loosely if needed; and no nose blowing for 24 hours after bleeding stops, so clots are not dislodged.
When it does not stop — cautery with silver nitrate or electrocautery and anterior packing. Posterior bleeding is more severe and harder to control, with greater risk of airway compromise: posterior packing, epistaxis catheters, or gel tampons, usually removed after 1–3 days. Observe for respiratory distress, assess patients on opioids at least hourly for gag and cough reflexes, and use oximetry. Refractory bleeding may need vessel ligation or embolization — whose complications include facial pain, skin or mucosal necrosis, facial nerve paralysis, and blindness.
Nasal fracture — document deviation, a malaligned bridge, changed nasal breathing, crepitus, bruising, and pain. Closed reduction within the first 24 hours, because after that edema and scar formation make it harder and reduction may be delayed for days.
The CSF test — clear drainage may be cerebrospinal fluid from a skull fracture. CSF contains glucose and tests positive on a dipstick, and dried on filter paper it leaves a yellow halo ring at the edge. This is rare and indicates serious injury.
Rhinoplasty and nasoseptoplasty — the patient returns with packing in both nostrils and cannot breathe through the nose, plus a "mustache" drip pad and often a splint. Assess how often the patient swallows — repeated swallowing signals posterior bleeding; examine the throat with a penlight. Semi-Fowler position, move slowly, cool compresses, at least 2500 mL/day once the gag reflex returns. Avoid forceful coughing, straining, sniffing upward, nose blowing, and sneezing with the mouth closed; avoid aspirin and NSAIDs. Final cosmetic result takes 6–12 months.
Facial trauma and the Le Fort classification — mandibular fractures are most common. Le Fort I is a nasoethmoid complex fracture; Le Fort II adds the maxilla; Le Fort III combines both plus an orbital-zygoma fracture — craniofacial disjunction, where the midface has no connection to the skull. The rich facial blood supply means extensive bleeding and bruising.
Assessment priorities in facial trauma — airway first: stridor, dyspnea, anxiety, restlessness, hypoxia, cyanosis, falling consciousness. Then bleeding and fractures, soft tissue edema, facial asymmetry, CSF leak from ears or nose, vision and eye movement (orbital and maxillary fractures entrap nerves and muscles), and bruising behind the ears — the battle sign, associated with skull fracture and brain trauma. Facial trauma co-occurs with spinal trauma, so cervical spine imaging is obtained.
Fixed occlusion and IMF — the jaws are wired closed for 6–10 weeks. Teach oral care with an electronic irrigating device and a dental liquid diet. Always keep wire cutters on hand: if the patient vomits they cannot open the jaws, so the wires are cut to prevent aspiration — then return to the surgeon for rewiring immediately.
Laryngeal trauma — from a crush or direct blow, fracture, or prolonged intubation. Difficulty breathing, aphonia (inability to produce sound), hoarseness, subcutaneous emphysema, and hemoptysis. Assess gas exchange and vital signs every 15–30 minutes; if signs of respiratory difficulty appear, stay with the patient and have the team prepare for emergency intubation or tracheotomy.
Why
Two of these rules invert what intuition suggests, which is precisely why they are tested. Abdominal thrusts stop the moment the patient becomes unconscious — you switch to CPR compressions even though you know an object is there — and a nosebleed patient leans forward rather than back, because tipping the head back hides the blood loss and sends it toward the airway. In both cases the instinctive action protects the appearance of the problem and endangers the patient.
Head and neck cancer — treatment and surgical care
The disease — about 4% of US cancers, usually squamous cell carcinoma, slow-growing and curable when found early. Chronic irritation thickens the mucosa, genes controlling growth are damaged, and lesions appear first as white patches (leukoplakia) or red velvety patches (erythroplakia). Spread is first to local nodes, muscle, and bone, later to lung and liver.
Risk factors — tobacco and alcohol are the two major ones; also voice abuse, chronic laryngitis, chemical and dust exposure, poor oral hygiene, long-term GERD, and oral HPV infection. More common in men and over age 50. Calculate pack-years and quantify alcohol in drinks per day and years.
Warning signs to teach — pain; a lump in the mouth, throat, or neck; difficulty swallowing; color change in the mouth or tongue to red, white, gray, dark brown, or black; an oral sore that does not heal in 2 weeks; unexplained oral bleeding; numbness of mouth, lips, or face; a change in denture fit; burning with citrus juice or hot liquids; persistent one-sided ear pain; hoarseness or voice change; recurrent sore throat; shortness of breath; anorexia and weight loss.
Nose and sinus cancer — rare, linked to chronic exposure to wood, textile, leather, and flour dusts, nickel and chromium, mustard gas, and radium, with smoking multiplying the risk. Onset is slow and the symptoms mimic sinusitis: persistent obstruction, drainage, bloody discharge, and pain that persists after sinusitis treatment.
Radiation — cure rate of at least 40% for small localized tumors. Expect hoarseness, sore throat, dysphagia, skin reaction, impaired taste, and dry mouth for weeks. Teach the patient to protect irradiated skin from sun, heat, cold, and shaving, wear soft cotton, and wash gently with mild soap. For xerostomia: sip cool fluids all day, sugar-free candy or gum, moisturizing sprays, humidification — and a dental consult, because dry mouth plus radiation sharply raises caries risk.
Chemotherapy and targeted therapy — usually cisplatin (or another platinum drug) with 5-FU, often given concurrently with radiation (chemoradiation). Cetuximab targets EGFR but also blocks it in normal tissue, so severe skin reactions are common and distressing. Nivolumab and pembrolizumab are used for some HPV-associated cancers.
Surgery and voice — from cord stripping and transoral cordectomy (normal or hoarse voice, high cure rate) through laryngofissure and supraglottic or hemilaryngectomy (hoarse), to total laryngectomy — larynx, hyoid, strap muscles, and one or two tracheal rings removed, leaving no natural voice and a permanent stoma. A neck dissection removes lymph nodes, the sternocleidomastoid, the jugular vein, the 11th cranial nerve, and soft tissue; shoulder drop is expected.
Before surgery — teach airway self-management, suctioning, pain control, the ICU environment, nutrition support and feeding tubes, and discharge plans. With the speech-language pathologist, establish and practice the communication method before surgery, while the patient can still speak.
After surgery — the operation often lasts 8+ hours and the patient recovers in ICU. Vital signs and hemorrhage checks hourly for the first 24 hours. A laryngectomy tube (shorter, wider than a tracheostomy tube) prevents shrinkage of the skin-tracheal border. Clean the suture line with sterile saline every 1–2 hours for the first days, then every 4 hours; healthy stoma mucosa is bright pink and shiny, like oral mucosa.
Flaps and wound breakdown — evaluate grafts and flaps hourly for the first 72 hours: capillary refill, color, drainage, and Doppler signal. Position so the flaps are not dependent. Wound breakdown is common, especially after preoperative radiation, and an extensive wound can leave the carotid artery exposed.
Carotid artery rupture — assess hourly for a leak. If a leak is suspected, call the Rapid Response Team and do not touch the area — pressure could cause immediate rupture. If it ruptures, apply constant direct manual pressure, secure the airway, do not leave the patient, and transport immediately to surgery. The risk of stroke and death is high.
The team — respiratory therapist for ventilation and tracheostomy teaching; dietitian, because patients lose an average of at least 20 lb after surgery and many are already depleted; speech-language pathologist for communication and ongoing swallow assessment.
Why
The instruction not to touch a suspected carotid leak runs against every reflex a nurse has, and it is the right one: a leaking vessel under a breaking-down wound is held together by very little, and palpation is enough to convert a leak into an exsanguinating rupture. Notice also that the communication plan is built before the operation — once the larynx is gone, the patient cannot tell you they are in distress, so the writing board is airway safety equipment, not a courtesy.
Obstructive sleep apnea
What it is — repeated apneic periods of 10 seconds or longer during sleep, from upper airway collapse. Each episode drops oxygen and jolts the sympathetic system awake.
Risk factors — obesity and a large neck circumference, large tonsils or uvula, a small or receding jaw, nasal obstruction, smoking, alcohol and sedatives at bedtime, male sex, and increasing age.
Cues — loud snoring with witnessed pauses, gasping arousals, morning headache, dry mouth, unrefreshing sleep, daytime sleepiness, irritability and poor concentration, and over time hypertension, atrial fibrillation, and right-sided heart failure.
Diagnosis — an overnight sleep studypoly- many · somn/o- sleep · -graphy recordingpoly·somno·graphy, which counts apneic and hypopneic episodes per hour (the AHI).
Management — weight loss, side-lying position, no alcohol or sedatives before bed, treat nasal obstruction; CPAP is the mainstay (a splint of positive pressure holding the airway open); oral appliances; surgery such as uvulopalatopharyngoplasty for selected patients.
Nursing priority — CPAP adherence is the whole battle: assess mask fit, skin breakdown, dry nose, claustrophobia, and bring the patient's own machine to the hospital. Be extremely cautious with opioids and sedatives, which worsen the collapse.
Why
OSA is a cardiovascular disease wearing a sleep costume: every apnea causes hypoxemia and a sympathetic surge, and repeated hundreds of times a night that becomes hypertension, dysrhythmias, and eventually right heart failure. It is also the reason a postoperative OSA patient is a monitoring priority — the drugs that relieve pain remove exactly the muscle tone that was holding the airway open.
Epistaxis, trauma, and obstruction
nosebleedepi- upon · -staxis drippingEpi·staxis first aid: sit upright and lean forward, pinch the soft part of the nose for 10–15 minutes continuously, apply ice to the nose and face, and keep the patient calm. Leaning back sends blood down the throat and hides how much is being lost.
If it will not stop — nasal packing or balloon, cautery, and sometimes arterial ligation. With posterior packing, monitor closely: airway obstruction, hypoxia, infection, and pain are all real risks, and humidification plus oral care keep the patient tolerable.
Teach after a bleed — no nose blowing, no straining or heavy lifting, no aspirin or NSAIDs, humidify the air, and use saline spray or water-soluble lubricant.
Facial and nasal trauma — the priority is always airway first, then bleeding, then the fracture. Watch for clear drainage that tests positive for glucose or forms a halo ring on gauze: that is cerebrospinal fluid and means a skull base fracture — do not pack, do not suction the nose, notify immediately.
surgical reshaping of the noserhin/o- nose · -plasty to reshapeRhino·plasty after care — head elevated, ice, expect edema and bruising, and watch for frequent swallowing, which is the sign of bleeding running down the back of the throat.
Upper airway obstruction — causes include the tongue in an unresponsive patient, aspiration of food or object, edema (anaphylaxis, burns, infection), tumor, and trauma. Cues: stridor, choking, gasping, wheezing, restlessness, cyanosis. Actions: call for help, position and open the airway, abdominal thrusts for a foreign body in a responsive adult, suction, prepare for emergency intubation or cricothyroidotomy.
Why
The recurring test point is "frequent swallowing." After any procedure in the nose or throat, blood does not appear on a dressing — it runs backward and the patient swallows it, so repeated swallowing is the earliest sign of hemorrhage. Lean-forward positioning exists for the same reason: it keeps blood where you can see and measure it instead of in the stomach, where it causes vomiting and aspiration.
Head and neck cancer, and the laryngectomy patient
Risk factors — tobacco in any form and alcohol, especially together; HPV infection; sun exposure for lip cancer; occupational exposures; poor oral hygiene and chronic irritation.
Warning signs to teach — hoarseness lasting more than 2 weeks, a lump in the neck or mouth, a sore that does not heal, difficulty or painful swallowing, unexplained ear pain, persistent unilateral nasal obstruction or bleeding.
Airway is the postoperative priority — airway and bleeding first, then pain, nutrition, and communication. Keep the head of the bed elevated, suction available, a spare tracheostomy tube and obturator at the bedside, and never remove a fresh airway.
Communication plan — establish it before surgery: writing board, picture board, tablet, and an agreed signal for distress. After healing, options include esophageal speech, an electrolarynx, and a tracheoesophageal puncture prosthesis.
Nutrition — swallowing evaluation before oral intake, upright positioning, thickened liquids or tube feeding as indicated, and monitoring weight and albumin.
Stoma care teaching — cover the stoma with a loose cloth or shield, humidify at home, no swimming and shower with care, protect from aerosols and dust, and wear medical alert identification stating "neck breather" — mouth-to-stoma rescue breathing is required if they ever arrest.
Why
A total laryngectomy permanently separates the airway from the mouth and nose, and almost every teaching point falls out of that one anatomical change: no humidification (so humidify), no filtering (so cover), no nose-holding for swimming (so never swim), and no air through the mouth (so rescue breathing goes to the stoma). Teach the anatomy once and the safety list is self-evident.
The nursing process for this chapter

The upper airway is one lumen with no backup, so every assessment in this chapter runs airway-first and every plan is built around the fact that swelling, blood, or an object can close it in minutes.

A — Assess
Airway, always first
Voice quality and ability to speak; stridor, drooling, or inability to swallow secretions; rising respiratory effort; retractions and seesaw motion; restlessness and a feeling of impending doom; saturation and ETCO₂. In partial obstruction the early signs are vague — diaphoresis, tachycardia, anxiety, rising blood pressure — so treat the whole cluster as airway until proven otherwise.
Sleep and breathing history
Snoring with witnessed pauses and gasping arousals, daytime sleepiness, morning headache, dry mouth, unrefreshing sleep, irritability and poor concentration. Screen with STOP-Bang or the Epworth scale. Examine the jaw, chin, neck, uvula, tonsils, and tongue, and note hypertension that does not respond as expected to therapy.
Bleeding and trauma
Site and estimated volume of epistaxis and whether it is anterior or posterior; frequent swallowing as the earliest sign of posterior or postoperative bleeding. In facial trauma: deviation, crepitus, asymmetry, vision and eye movement, battle sign, and clear drainage that is glucose-positive or leaves a halo ring. Assume cervical spine involvement.
Cancer and stoma
Warning signs lasting beyond 2 weeks — hoarseness, a neck or oral lump, a nonhealing sore, dysphagia, one-sided ear pain. Quantify tobacco in pack-years and alcohol in drinks. For a stoma or fresh flap: mucosa color, secretion character and thickness, flap capillary refill and Doppler signal, and the suture line.
Why
Restlessness and anxiety are listed as airway findings rather than emotional ones on purpose. In a narrowing upper airway they are the earliest reliable cue, and the intuitive response — reassurance, or worse, sedation — removes the muscle tone and the report that were keeping the patient alive.
D — Diagnose & prioritize
1. Potential for airway obstruction related to edema, secretions, bleeding, a foreign body, or loss of tone — evidenced by stridor, retractions, restlessness, or falling saturation. This outranks everything in this chapter, every time.
2. Potential for hemorrhage related to a vascular surgical field or nasal vessels — evidenced by frequent swallowing, bright red bleeding, or a falling pressure with rising pulse.
3. Decreased gas exchange related to apneic episodes or obstruction.
4. Impaired verbal communication related to laryngectomy, tracheostomy, or wired jaws — a safety problem, not a comfort one.
5. Inadequate nutrition related to dysphagia, oral surgery, or fixed occlusion.
6. Disturbed body image and grieving related to permanent change in voice, breathing, smell, and appearance.
Why
Communication ranks above nutrition and body image because a patient who cannot speak cannot report the airway problem that is first on the list. The writing board is airway equipment. Body image is a genuine priority in this chapter and still never outranks a threatened airway.
P — Plan
Airway remains patent; no stridor, no unrelieved obstruction, and no episode requiring emergency intubation.
For OSA: CPAP used nightly including naps, with an adherent mask fit and intact skin; postoperative patients monitored continuously while receiving opioids or sedatives.
Bleeding controlled; no unrecognized posterior bleed — the patient and staff both watching for frequent swallowing.
A communication method is established before surgery and demonstrated back, with a distress signal agreed and the call light always within reach.
Swallowing evaluated before oral intake; weight and nutritional markers stable.
Before discharge the patient states stoma or wound care, humidification needs, the no-blowing and no-straining rules, and which findings require immediate return.
Why
The communication outcome carries a deadline — before surgery — because afterward the patient cannot participate in building the plan. Teaching a writing board to someone who has already lost their voice is remediation, not preparation.
I — Implement
Independent
Keep suction, a spare tube of the same size and one smaller, an obturator, and a manual resuscitation bag at the bedside of any new airway. Never remove a fresh tracheostomy tube. Meticulous oral care — crusted secretions are a preventable cause of fatal obstruction. Continuous humidification for a stoma. Suction sterilely, preoxygenate, 10–15 seconds, suction on withdrawal only. Upright positioning; ice and pressure for epistaxis with the patient leaning forward. Keep wire cutters at the bedside for fixed occlusion.
Dependent
Oxygen and humidification; scheduled (not PRN) analgesia for the richly innervated oropharynx, avoiding aspirin and NSAIDs in the first 24 hours; antiemetics; packing, cautery, or embolization for refractory bleeding; enteral feeding or thickened liquids after a swallow study; antibiotics and wound care.
Emergency actions
Obstruction: call for help, open and position the airway, insert an oral or nasal airway, suction, abdominal thrusts in the responsive adult — but chest compressions once unresponsive, even with a known foreign body. Prepare for intubation before swelling closes the option, or cricothyroidotomy. Suspected carotid leak after neck surgery: call the Rapid Response Team and do not touch the area; if it ruptures, constant direct pressure, secure the airway, do not leave the patient.
Collaborative
Speech-language pathology for the communication plan and swallow assessments; respiratory therapy; dietitian — head and neck surgery patients lose an average of at least 20 lb; sleep medicine and CPAP supply; smoking and alcohol cessation; social work and support groups.
Why
Two instructions here override reflex. You stop abdominal thrusts the moment the patient goes unconscious and start compressions instead, and you do not palpate a suspected carotid leak. Both feel wrong and both are right: the first because compressions generate better airway pressure and circulate blood, the second because pressure on a breaking-down vessel converts a leak into an exsanguination.
E — Evaluate
Airway patency maintained throughout, with no emergency airway event. Breathing effort, saturation, and ETCO₂ stable. Bleeding stopped and no recurrence with the teaching in place. The patient communicates needs reliably by the agreed method and uses the call light. Swallowing is safe, intake adequate, weight stable. Stoma mucosa stays bright pink and shiny, secretions stay thin, and the patient or family demonstrates care, suctioning, and humidification.
Unmet looks like: new or worsening stridor, drooling, rising respiratory effort, falling saturation or rising ETCO₂, a shrinking oropharynx after surgery, frequent swallowing or bright red bleeding, a dusky or poorly perfused flap, or any suspicion of a carotid leak.
Why
Evaluation in this chapter is mostly done at the bedside in the first 24 hours, on a clock: hourly bleeding checks, every 2 hours for airway narrowing after airway surgery, hourly for 72 hours on flaps. The interval is the evaluation — problems here declare themselves in minutes, not shifts.
Chapter 23 check Test this chapter now — questions first, answers and rationales after. Recall questions NCLEX set Missed concepts
24 · Chapter 24
Gas exchange · perfusion · inflammation · cellular regulation

Noninfectious lower respiratory conditions

On Exam 2 · 8 questionsAsthma·COPD

On examIntro. Below the larynx the airway stops being one lumen and becomes a tree — two mainstem bronchi, five secondary bronchi, thousands of bronchioles, then alveolar ducts and alveoli. Nothing here closes in minutes, so the chapter is not about speed; it is about chronic, progressive loss. Every disease in it narrows the tube (asthma, chronic bronchitis), destroys the elastic sac (emphysema), thickens the membrane (fibrosis), plugs it with thick mucus (cystic fibrosis), squeezes the vessel (pulmonary arterial hypertension), or fills the space with tumor (lung cancer). Name which of those six is happening and the assessment findings, the drug class, and the nursing priority all follow.

Connection Map How this chapter fits together
1 · Normal physiologyMillions of small airways and alveoli exchange gas across a thin, elastic membrane.
2 · What failsAirways narrow (asthma, COPD), alveoli are destroyed (emphysema), secretions thicken (CF), vessels constrict (PAH), or the membrane scars (IPF).
3 · What the nurse seesWheeze, prolonged exhalation, barrel chest, pursed-lip breathing, clubbing, JVD in right heart strain.
4 · What proves itPFTs (FEV₁/FVC; ≥12% bronchodilator response = asthma), ABGs, sweat chloride, right heart catheterization, HRCT.
5 · What targets itSABA opens airways now; ICS calms inflammation over time; oxygen to target; airway clearance; PAH vasodilators.
6 · If it worsensAir trapping → fatigue → CO₂ retention; chronic hypoxia → cor pulmonale.
7 · Emergency triggerSilent chest, drowsiness with rising PaCO₂, chest tube drainage >100 mL/hr, tracheal deviation, interrupted epoprostenol.
Cause and effect
Airway narrowing→wheeze and prolonged exhalation→air trapping→rising work of breathing→fatigue and rising CO₂→silent chest is an emergency
Beta2 agonist→bronchial smooth muscle relaxes→airways widen→wheeze improves→tremor and tachycardia because beta receptors outside the lung are stimulated too
On exam
Asthma vs COPD
Asthma is reversible and episodic; COPD is progressive and poorly reversible.
SABA vs ICS
SABA = rescue in minutes. ICS = controller over weeks; rinse mouth to prevent thrush.
On exam
Why the drug makes sense
ICS → local immune suppression → oral thrush. LABA alone in asthma → symptoms masked while inflammation worsens → must pair with ICS. Epoprostenol half-life minutes → interruption = rebound PAH. Drug cards →
Connections to other chapters
Ch 22 PFTs and ABGs; ch 29 right-sided failure (cor pulmonale); ch 34 polycythemia from chronic hypoxia.
Monitor
SpO₂ at ordered target (often 88–92% in CO₂ retainers), stable tidaling in the water seal.
Act now
Silent chest, PaCO₂ rising with confusion, continuous bubbling with new distress, facial swelling (SVC syndrome).
Down to the anatomy and physiology
The tree — trachea → two mainstem bronchi → five secondary bronchi → thousands of bronchi and bronchioles → alveolar ducts → alveoli, where gas exchange happens. Air must flow through the entire tubular system for oxygen to reach the exchanging surface, so a problem anywhere along it impairs both gas exchange and systemic perfusion.
Elastic recoil is what empties the lung. Exhalation is passive: stretched elastin pulls the alveoli back down. Destroy elastin and the lung inflates but will not deflate — air is trapped, the alveoli become large flabby enlarged air-filled spaces where alveoli have mergedbulla a bubble · plural bullaebullae, and surface area for exchange falls.
On examProteases versus elastin — proteases normally destroy inhaled particulates. Cigarette smoke drives protease synthesis far above normal, and the excess breaks down elastin itself. Alpha₁-antitrypsin is the brake on proteases; inherited AAT deficiency removes the brake, which is why emphysema in a non-smoker or before age 40 points to genetics.
The flattened diaphragm — a hyperinflated lung pushes the diaphragm down and flat, and a flat muscle cannot generate the same pull. The patient recruits neck, chest wall, and abdominal accessory muscles, which burn more oxygen for less air. Inhalation starts before exhalation finishes, producing the uncoordinated pattern and the sensation of air hunger.
The pressure chain to the heart — trapped air, collapsed airways, and stiff alveolar walls raise lung tissue pressure and narrow the pulmonary vessels. The right ventricle, built thin for a low-pressure circuit, must generate high pressures. It hypertrophies and then fails: right-sided heart failure caused by lung diseasecor heart · pulmonale of the lungcor pulmonale. Normal pulmonary pressure is 15–18 mm Hg; hypertension begins above 25.
Why CO₂ retention is the late sign — CO₂ diffuses far more easily than oxygen, so hypoxemia comes first and the patient compensates by raising the rate. ABGs can look near-normal until disease is advanced. Then CO₂ is produced faster than it can be blown off: chronic high blood carbon dioxidehyper- above · capnia smoke, vapour — carbon dioxidehyper·capnia with respiratory acidosis, and the kidney retains bicarbonate as compensation.
Why
Lower airway = millions of parallel paths, so losing some is survivable and deterioration is measured in months and years rather than minutes. That single structural fact inverts the nursing emphasis from chapter 23: here the trend matters more than any single finding, the patient's own baseline is the reference value, and the work of breathing — not the saturation number — is what tells you whether the disease is winning.
On exam
Asthma — the full picture
Definition — a chronic disease in which reversible acute airway obstruction occurs intermittently, reducing airflow. Two mechanisms run together: inflammation narrows the lumen from the inside, and airway hyperresponsiveness constricts bronchial smooth muscle from the outside. Most adults have both — which is why two drug classes are needed. More than 3500 deaths a year in the United States; over 20 million adults affected, and about half also had it as children.
Two inflammatory types — eosinophilic asthma (about 50% of cases) is driven by IL-5; neutrophilic asthma is driven by IL-17. Symptoms are identical; the long-term effect on the epithelium differs, and some drugs are specific to one type — which is why the monoclonals are targeted by mediator.
Triggers and history — intermittent dyspnea, chest tightness, cough, wheeze, and increased mucus. Ask whether symptoms are continuous, seasonal, activity- or work-related, or worse at night; some patients have symptoms for 4–8 weeks after a cold. Atopic patients usually have other allergies. Wheezing in a non-smoker is important diagnostically.
Control level (GINA) — four questions: daytime symptoms more than twice weekly, night waking with symptoms, reliever needed more than twice weekly, activity limited by symptoms. None = controlled · 1–2 = partly controlled · 3–4 = uncontrolled.
During an attack — audible wheeze, louder on exhalation at first, prolonged exhalation, accessory muscle use with retractions at the sternum, suprasternal notch and between the ribs, inability to speak more than a few words between breaths. Long-standing severe disease gives a barrel chest: the normal AP:lateral ratio of 1:1.5 becomes 1:1 from chronic air trapping, with a flattened diaphragm and widened rib spaces.
Labs — PaO₂ falls; PaCO₂ falls early (blowing it off), then rises late with end-tidal CO₂ as retention begins. Allergic asthma shows raised serum eosinophils and IgE; sputum may contain eosinophils and Curschmann spirals (mucous plugs with shed epithelial cells).
PFTs are the diagnosis — FVC, FEV₁ and peak expiratory flow. Asthma is diagnosed when values increase by 12% or more after a bronchodilator — reversibility is the whole point. Responsiveness can be provoked with inhaled methacholine, which induces bronchospasm in susceptible adults.
Reliever versus control — SABA (albuterol, levalbuterol, terbutaline) stops an attack and premedicates before trigger activity; always carry it and check it still holds doses. LABA (salmeterol, formoterol) prevents but cannot stop an attack, needs days to build, and carries an FDA black box warning: never as sole therapy. Cholinergic antagonists / LAMAs (ipratropium short-acting, tiotropium once daily) bronchodilate and dry secretions. Methylxanthines (theophylline) are a last resort — therapeutic dose sits close to the toxic one, so blood levels are monitored.
Antiinflammatories are control drugs only — inhaled corticosteroids are the most effective (fluticasone, budesonide, mometasone); systemic steroids are short-term for moderate disease and daily only in severe. Cromolyn blocks mast cell membranes; nedocromil inhibits mediator release. Leukotriene modifiers: montelukast and zafirlukast block the receptor, zileuton blocks synthesis. Monoclonals — omalizumab (anti-IgE), dupilumab (IL-4 receptor), benralizumab/mepolizumab/reslizumab (IL-5) — and none are for an acute attack.
Inhaler technique is examinable — MDI: shake 5 seconds, tongue under the mouthpiece, inhale slowly and deeply, hold 5–10 seconds; spacer if timing is hard. DPI: exhale away from the device, inhale quickly and deeply over 2–3 seconds, hold 4–10 seconds, and never wash it with water. SMI: prime three times before first use and again after 3 days unused, don't cover the side vents, hold 10 seconds.
Action plan and self-management — the plan carries the daily controller schedule, reliever directions, personal control questions, how to step the controller up, when to call, and emergency actions. Add a symptom diary and peak-flow self-monitoring during exacerbations, after a treatment change, and whenever symptoms worsen. Exercise is recommended; adjust the routine or premedicate with a SABA rather than stopping.
Status asthmaticus — a severe life-threatening episode that intensifies once it begins and does not respond to usual therapy: extreme labored breathing, wheezing, accessory muscle use, distended neck veins. IV fluids, systemic bronchodilators, steroids, epinephrine and oxygen immediately; prepare for emergency intubation. Sudden absence of wheezing with a low saturation means complete obstruction and calls for a tracheotomy. Untreated it ends in pneumothorax and cardiac or respiratory arrest.
Why
Every asthma drug error on an exam comes from one confusion: bronchodilators open the tube, antiinflammatories calm the lining, and neither does the other's job. That is why a patient with both bronchospasm and inflammation needs two classes, why a steroid inhaler is useless in an attack, and why a LABA alone kills — it masks worsening inflammation while doing nothing about it. And notice the silent chest: a wheeze requires moving air, so its disappearance is deterioration, not improvement.
On exam
COPD — the gas exchange concept exemplar
What it is — a collection of lower airway disorders that interfere with airflow and gas exchange, comprising emphysema and chronic bronchitis. They are separate pathologies and most patients have both. Almost 15.7 million Americans diagnosed; roughly 3 million deaths a year worldwide.
Emphysema — the sac — destruction of elastic tissue reduces recoil, so the lung hyperinflates. Alveoli are destroyed or become large and flabby; air is trapped; the diaphragm flattens; accessory muscles take over. Primary emphysema is AAT deficiency (suspect it in non-smokers or onset before 40); secondary emphysema is mainly cigarette smoke, plus pollution, occupational exposure, and childhood respiratory infection. Late disease: CO₂ retention with chronic respiratory acidosis and a low PaO₂, and a decreased diffusion capacity because the surface area is gone.
Chronic bronchitis — the tube — inflammation of bronchi and bronchioles with increased mucus and a chronic productive cough at least 3 months a year for 2 consecutive years. Irritants trigger inflammation, vasodilation, mucosal edema, congestion and bronchospasm; mucus glands grow in number and size; walls thicken. Mucus is a breeding ground, so chronic infection follows. Diffusion capacity in bronchitis alone is usually normal.
Risk — smoking is the greatest risk factor, and a 20 pack-year history or longer usually already shows PFT changes. Ask about e-cigarettes and vaping too. Asthma is itself a risk factor: COPD incidence is 12 times greater in adults with asthma, and the overlap is named asthma-COPD overlap syndrome. Seen more often in women and in adults 65 and over.
Complications — hypoxemia and acidosis, respiratory infection, cardiac failure (especially cor pulmonale), dysrhythmias (from hypoxemia, drugs, acidosis), and respiratory failure. Cor pulmonale key features: hypoxia and hypoxemia, increasing dyspnea, fatigue, an enlarged tender liver, warm cyanotic hands and feet with bounding pulses, cyanotic lips, distended neck veins, right ventricular hypertrophy, visible pulsations below the sternum, nausea or anorexia, dependent edema, metabolic and respiratory acidosis, pulmonary hypertension.
What the patient looks like — thin, muscle mass lost in the extremities but neck muscles enlarged, slow-moving, slightly stooped, sitting forward with arms braced in the orthopneic (tripod) position. Rapid shallow breathing, jerky uncoordinated respiratory movement, abdominal wall sucked in on inspiration; 40–50 breaths/min in an exacerbation demands immediate attention. Limited diaphragmatic excursion, decreased fremitus, hyperresonance on percussion, reduced breath sounds, wheezes on inspiration and expiration but crackles usually absent. A silent chest means serious obstruction or pneumothorax. Chronic bronchitis adds a dusky cyanotic appearance, copious sputum, delayed capillary refill and finger clubbing.
GOLD classification — by FEV₁ % predicted · 1 Mild ≥80% · 2 Moderate ≤50 to <80% · 3 Severe ≤30 to <50% · 4 Very severe <30%. PFTs are run before and after a bronchodilator; residual volume is the most affected volume, and the FEV₁/FVC ratio falls as disease progresses. The CAT (eight items, 0–5 each, score 0–40) adds an A–D symptom designation, because a GOLD 4 patient can be an A (low exacerbation risk) and a GOLD 1 patient a D (high risk).
Other labs — ABGs then oximetry for trend; polycythemia (raised Hgb/Hct) from chronic hypoxia; electrolytes, because low phosphate, potassium, calcium and magnesium reduce muscle strength; WBC and sputum culture with infection; serum AAT if there is a family history. Saturation often runs well below 90%, and patients usually know their own usual number — a change from their baseline is what requires attention.
Priority problems — 1. decreased gas exchange; 2. weight loss from dyspnea-limited intake; 3. fatigue from an oxygen supply-demand imbalance; 4. potential for pneumonia and other respiratory infections.
Interventions — airway maintenance first — assess rate, rhythm, depth and accessory muscle use before any intervention, and look for a contributing infection. Same drug classes as asthma, weighted to long-acting agents and combinations (fluticasone/vilanterol; fluticasone/umeclidinium/vilanterol), plus mucolytics (nebulised saline or acetylcysteine, oral guaifenesin). Stepped therapy: up with exacerbations, down when stable. Have the patient demonstrate inhaler technique at every visit.
