33 · Chapter 33
Clotting · perfusion · immunity

Assessment of the hematologic system

Not on the study guideNo questions listed for this chapter — lowest priority.

Intro. Blood does three jobs: it carries oxygen (red cells), it defends (white cells), and it seals leaks (platelets and clotting factors). Every finding in this chapter and the next is one of those three failing — too few, too many, or malformed.

Connection Map How this chapter fits together
1 · Normal physiologyMarrow makes red cells (carry), white cells (defend) and platelets (clot).
2 · What failsProduction, survival or function of a cell line fails.
3 · What the nurse seesFatigue, pallor and tachycardia (RBC); infection (WBC); petechiae and bleeding (platelets).
4 · What proves itCBC, reticulocytes, iron studies, B₁₂/folate, PT/INR, aPTT, bone marrow biopsy.
5 · What targets itReplace what is missing and protect the client from the consequence.
6 · If it worsensHypoxia, sepsis, hemorrhage.
7 · Emergency triggerNeutropenic fever, platelets <20,000 with bleeding, Hgb falling with tachycardia and hypotension.
Cause and effect
Low hemoglobin→less oxygen content→heart rate rises to compensate→fatigue and dyspnea on exertion→SpO₂ can still read normal
PT/INR vs aPTT
INR tracks warfarin (extrinsic). aPTT tracks heparin (intrinsic).
Why the drug makes sense
Aspirin/NSAIDs/clopidogrel impair platelets; chemotherapy suppresses marrow → counts fall → bleeding and infection risk. Drug cards →
Connections to other chapters
Ch 22 oximetry limits; ch 30 anticoagulant labs; ch 25 infection risk in neutropenia.
Monitor
Counts stable, no bleeding or fever.
Act now
Fever with ANC <500, active bleeding, sudden Hgb drop.
Down to the cell
Blood stem cells in red marrow can become any blood cell. Marrow lives mainly in the flat bones — sternum, ribs, pelvis, vertebrae — which is where biopsies come from.
making red blood cellserythr/o- red · -poiesis makingErythro·poiesis — low tissue oxygen triggers the kidney to release erythropoietin, which tells marrow to make red cells. Needs iron, vitamin B₁₂, and folate as raw material. Red cells live about 120 days.
The spleen and liver remove aged cells, recycle iron, and store platelets. The liver also makes most clotting factors and needs vitamin K to do it — which is why liver disease causes bleeding.
the controlled process of stopping bleedinghem/o- blood · -stasis standing stillHemo·stasis — three steps: vessel spasm, the platelet plug, then the coagulation cascade laying fibrin over it. dissolving the clot once the vessel is repairedfibrin the clot protein · -lysis breaking downFibrino·lysis then trims the clot so it does not overgrow.
Aging — marrow becomes fattier with less reserve, so recovery from blood loss is slower; hemoglobin drifts lower; and the immune and clotting responses are blunted.
Why
The 120-day red cell life span explains two exam staples: why A1C reports a three-month average, and why a patient who starts iron today will not feel better for weeks — you have to grow new cells, not repair old ones. And the kidney's role in erythropoietin is why chronic kidney disease causes anemia that iron alone will not fix.
Assessing blood without seeing it
History — fatigue and exercise tolerance, bruising or bleeding (gums, nose, stool, urine, menses), frequent or severe infections, family history of anemia or clotting disorders, diet (iron, B₁₂, folate, alcohol), drugs (anticoagulants, aspirin, NSAIDs, chemotherapy), and occupational or radiation exposure.
Skin and mucous membranes — pallor (check conjunctivae, palms, and oral mucosa in darker skin), jaundice from hemolysis, pinpoint bleeds under the skinpetechia a small spotpetechiae, purpura and ecchymoses, and poor healing.
Head to toe — smooth sore tongue and spoon nails in iron deficiency; beefy red tongue and neurologic changes in B₁₂ deficiency; bone or joint pain; enlarged lymph nodes, liver, or spleen; tachycardia and a systolic flow murmur in anemia.
Key labs — CBC with differential, reticulocyte count (is marrow responding?), iron studies and ferritin, B₁₂ and folate, PT/INR, aPTT, D-dimer, fibrinogen, Coombs test, hemoglobin electrophoresis for sickle cell and thalassemia, and peripheral smear.
Bone marrow aspiration and biopsy — consent, usually the posterior iliac crest, local anesthetic with brief sharp pressure and pain. After: pressure dressing, monitor for bleeding and infection, mild analgesia, avoid strenuous activity for 24 hours.
Why
The reticulocyte count is the single most diagnostic number here, because it answers "can the marrow respond?" A low hemoglobin with a high reticulocyte count means the marrow is working and cells are being lost or destroyed; with a low count it means production itself has failed. Same anemia, opposite causes, completely different treatment.
Hemostasis — the cascade and the brakes on it
Where cells come from — a pluripotent stem cell divides into a committed myeloid line (platelets, erythrocytes, and the myeloblast line producing neutrophils, eosinophils, basophils, and macrophages) and a committed lymphoid line (T lymphocytes, natural killer cells, and B lymphocyte plasma cells). The red cell pathway runs proerythroblast → basophilic → polychromatic → orthochromatic erythroblast → reticulocyte → erythrocyte.
The spleen — white pulp is filled with white blood cells and serves immunity; red pulp contains macrophages that remove aging, damaged, and dead red cells, antigens, and microorganisms, and stores functional red cells and platelets. Anyone who has had a splenectomy has reduced immune function and increased risk for infection and sepsis.
The liver — produces prothrombin and other clotting factors, and proper liver function is needed to form vitamin K in the intestinal tract — which is required for factors VII, IX, X, and prothrombin. It also stores large amounts of whole blood and blood cells, and stores extra iron as ferritin.
Hemostasis defined — controlled, localized clotting in damaged vessels that prevents excessive blood loss while perfusion continues everywhere else. It balances proclotting against anticlotting action through three sequential processes: platelet aggregation with plug formation, the clotting cascade, and fibrin clot formation.
Platelet plugs are not clots — platelets circulate individually until activated, at which point their membranes become sticky and they clump into large semisolid plugs that disrupt local flow. These plugs last only a few hours and cannot provide complete hemostasis; they only start the process.
What activates platelets — ADP, calcium, thromboxane A2, and collagen. Platelets secrete some of these themselves, so they can continue their own activation; others come from outside. Too few platelets means impaired clotting and excessive bleeding risk.
The extrinsic pathway — generally protective, triggered by factors outside the blood. The most common event is trauma that damages vessels and exposes collagen in the vessel wall, which activates platelets into a plug within seconds. It starts the cascade sooner because some intrinsic steps are bypassed. Also triggered by inflammation, bacterial toxins, or foreign proteins.
The intrinsic pathway — triggered by internal conditions: impaired flow (venous stasis) or excessive clot-promoting substances within the blood itself. The intrinsic pathway is the one that produces abnormal clotting. Both pathways merge into a common path ending in the same fibrin clot.
Why a cascade is hard to stop — it works like a landslide: a few stones dislodge large rocks and trees until an enormous mass is moving. Each clotting factor is an inactive enzyme activated in sequence by the one before it, and every step needs clotting factors, calcium, and more platelets. Factor numbers reflect the order of discovery, not the order of action.
The last two critical steps — activation of thrombin from prothrombin, and thrombin’s conversion of fibrinogen into fibrin. Only fibrin molecules can begin a true clot.
Building the clot — fibrinogen is an inactive liver protein; thrombin removes its end portions so active fibrin molecules link into fibrin threads forming a meshlike base. Factor XIII tightens the mesh, making it dense and stable; more platelets stick to the threads and attract other cells and proteins, and as the clot tightens the serum is squeezed out and formation is complete.
The factors worth knowing individually — I fibrinogen, converted to fibrin by thrombin. II prothrombin, vitamin K dependent. III tissue thromboplastin, starting the extrinsic cascade with factor VII. IV calcium, a cofactor for most clotting enzymes that also enhances platelet aggregation. There is no factor VI — it is an artifact. VII proconvertin, vitamin K dependent. VIII antihemophilic factor, which combines with von Willebrand factor to help platelets adhere — its lack causes hemophilia A. IX Christmas factor, vitamin K dependent, its lack causing hemophilia B. X Stuart-Prower, vitamin K dependent, converting prothrombin to thrombin. XI, whose deficiency causes mild bleeding. XII Hageman, critical to the intrinsic pathway. XIII fibrin-stabilizing factor, cross-linking the threads.
Why anticlotting forces exist — in theory a cascade would keep going until all the blood in the body had coagulated and perfusion stopped, which would be fatal. So whenever clotting begins, anticlotting begins too, limiting the clot to the damaged area while normal perfusion continues everywhere else.
Fibrinolysis — dissolves the edges of the fibrin clot to prevent overenlargement. Plasminogen is activated to plasmin, and plasmin digests fibrin, fibrinogen, and prothrombin, controlling clot size and producing fibrin degradation products.
The three anticlotting substances — protein C and protein S increase breakdown of factors V and VIII; antithrombin III inactivates thrombin and factors IX and X. Deficiency of any anticlotting factor increases the risk of VTE, pulmonary embolism, MI, and stroke.
Anticoagulants versus fibrinolytics — anticoagulants interfere with steps in the cascade to prevent new clots and limit extension of formed ones; they do not break down existing clots, and they do not change blood viscosity despite the nickname “blood thinners.” They are classed as platelet inhibitors, direct thrombin inhibitors, indirect thrombin inhibitors, and vitamin K antagonists. Fibrinolytics (alteplase, reteplase, tenecteplase) selectively break down fibrin threads in formed clots by activating plasminogen to plasmin.
Why
The single most clarifying line in this chapter is that anticoagulants do not dissolve clots. They stop the landslide from recruiting more of the hillside while the body’s own fibrinolysis works on what has already fallen. That is why a patient on heparin still has their DVT, why the leg is still not massaged, and why the treatment goal is stated as preventing the next event rather than curing the present one.
The hematologic history — drugs, genes, and diet
Start with age and sex — marrow function and immune activity decrease with age, and women biologically have lower red cell counts than men, with counts falling further during menstruation. Teach patients who menstruate to report changes in their cycle, especially with difficulty breathing, dizziness, fatigue, or weakness — these can indicate anemia.
Past history — known immunity, liver, or hematologic disorders; cancer, and specifically whether radiation therapy was used, since irradiating marrow-forming bones impairs hematologic function.
Environment and work — occupation, hobbies, and whether the home is near an industrial setting, to identify exposure to agents that affect bone marrow.
The COVID-19 questions — check vaccination status and infection history. If the patient received the Johnson & Johnson/Janssen or AstraZeneca vaccine 4 to 42 days before symptom onset, ask about severe headache, visual change, abdominal pain, nausea and vomiting, back pain, shortness of breath, leg pain or swelling, and petechiae, easy bruising, or easy bleeding.
VITT — vaccine-induced thrombotic thrombocytopenia, a syndrome of simultaneous acute thrombosis and thrombocytopenia. Rare, and often treatable with IV immunoglobulin and nonheparin anticoagulants, but delayed recognition can be fatal — so report positive findings right away. Workup: CBC with platelet count and peripheral smear, D-dimer, fibrinogen, a PF-4 heparin ELISA, and imaging.
Drugs causing marrow suppression — allopurinol, azathioprine, almost all chemotherapeutic agents, ciprofloxacin, colchicine, etanercept, hydroxychloroquine, interferon alfa, methotrexate, sulfamethoxazole/trimethoprim, and zidovudine. Also note whether the patient takes trilaciclib, given to reduce chemotherapy-induced marrow suppression.
Drugs causing hemolytic anemia — cephalosporins, dapsone, levodopa, levofloxacin, methyldopa, nitrofurantoin, NSAIDs, penicillin and its derivatives, phenazopyridine, and quinidine.
Drugs causing thrombocytopenia — a long list worth recognizing by pattern: acetaminophen, amiodarone, aspirin, beta-lactam antibiotics, carbamazepine, ceftriaxone, cetirizine, dexamethasone, diltiazem, furosemide, gold compounds (even after the drug is stopped), haloperidol, heparin, ibuprofen, linezolid, naproxen, oseltamivir, phenytoin, piperacillin, quinidine, quinine, ranitidine, rifampin, simvastatin and statins, sulfamethoxazole/trimethoprim, sulfonamides, valproic acid, and vancomycin.
Nutrition — diet alters cell quality, clotting, and overall hematologic health, and diets high in fat, salt, and sugar are linked to anemia. Ask what they have eaten in the past week and about supplements, then collaborate with a dietitian on possible iron, protein, mineral, or vitamin deficiencies.
Alcohol — chronic misuse causes nutritional deficiencies, damages the liver, and increases the risk of both bleeding and thrombosis.
Social determinants — ask about finances, social support, and where the patient lives. Reduced income or living in a food desert can mean a diet deficient in iron and protein, because those foods are more expensive or simply unavailable.
Family history — many blood and clotting disorders are inherited, especially those affecting clotting factors and anticlotting substances. Ask whether anyone has had hemophilia, frequent nosebleeds, postpartum hemorrhage, excessive bleeding after tooth extraction, or heavy bruising after mild trauma — and also about conditions of excessive clotting, which point to an inherited intrinsic-pathway problem.
Inherited conditions to ask about by name — sickle cell disease and sickle cell trait (seen often among Black and African American people in North America, but anyone can carry the trait), anemia, thalassemia, hemophilia, von Willebrand disease, and factor deficiencies including II, V, VII, XI, and XIII.
Current problems — lymph node swelling; excessive bruising or bleeding and whether it was spontaneous or after trauma; bleeding after routine dental work and how long it lasted. Have patients estimate pads or tampons used in the last cycle and whether that is a change, and whether clots are present — sizing them against common objects such as a quarter or a walnut.
Systemic symptoms — shortness of breath on exertion, palpitations, frequent infections, fevers, recent weight loss, headaches, and paresthesias; any combination can occur with a hematologic disorder.
Why fatigue is the most common symptom of anemia — cells use oxygen to make ATP, the chemical that powers most cellular work. Less oxygen delivered means less cellular work and more fatigue. Ask about feeling tired, needing more rest, or losing endurance during normal activities, and have them compare this month with a year ago. Also ask about vertigo, tinnitus, and an inflamed, swollen, or smooth red tongue.
Why
The drug history is the highest-yield part of this assessment because so much hematologic disease is iatrogenic. Everyday drugs — ibuprofen, furosemide, a statin, an antibiotic — appear on these lists, and gold compounds can cause thrombocytopenia after the drug has been stopped. So the question is not just what the patient takes now but what they have taken in the past month, and the answer can be the whole diagnosis.
Head-to-toe assessment, aging adaptations, and tests
Assess gently — examine any patient with, or suspected of having, a hematologic condition carefully enough to avoid causing bruising, petechiae, or bleeding. After venipuncture, hold pressure until bleeding stops, which can take several minutes.
Skin and mucous membranes — inspect for pallor or jaundice, and nail beds for pallor or cyanosis. Pallor may look whitish, bluish, or ash gray depending on the patient’s normal tone. Also assess the gums, conjunctivae, and palmar creases with the palm stretched — pallor there indicates decreased hemoglobin and poor oxygenation.
Health equity in skin assessment — do not rely on skin alone. White skin with pallor appears lighter than baseline; black or very dark brown skin with pallor appears ash gray; brown skin with pallor appears yellowish brown. Regularly inspect mucous membranes, nail beds, gums, conjunctivae, and palmar creases.
Bleeding survey — check the gums for bleeding in response to light pressure or a soft-bristled brush; inspect for petechiae (reddish-purple pinpoint hemorrhagic lesions) and ecchymoses, which may cluster together. In hospitalized patients, check for bleeding around nasogastric tubes, endotracheal tubes, central lines, peripheral IVs, and catheters. Check turgor and ask about itching, since dry skin from poor perfusion itches.
Head and neck — pallor, ulceration, and fissures at the corners of the mouth; tongue changes such as smoothness, beefy redness, or swelling in pernicious or iron deficiency anemia. For patients on chemotherapy or radiation, use an evidence-based tool such as the Oral Mucositis Risk Assessment Scale in Hematology Patients, so prevention starts sooner. Inspect and palpate all lymph node areas, documenting location, size, whether palpation hurts, and whether the node is fixed or mobile.
Respiratory — when oxygen delivery falls, the lungs work harder. Assess rate and depth at rest and during and after mild activity such as walking 20 steps in 10 seconds, and note whether the patient can complete a 10-word sentence without stopping for breath. Ask about easy fatigue, shortness of breath at rest or on exertion, and needing extra pillows to sleep.
Cardiovascular — the heart works harder too: pulses may become weak and thready. Observe for distended neck veins, edema, or phlebitis; listen for abnormal sounds and irregular rhythms; obtain a blood pressure.
Kidney and urinary — bleeding can cause hematuria, seen as grossly bloody red, pink, or cola-colored urine. Test for protein with a dipstick, since blood contains protein. Remember that chronic kidney disease produces less erythropoietin and is often anemic, and acute kidney injury can cause anemia from bleeding or hemolysis.
Musculoskeletal — rib or sternal tenderness can occur in some leukemias when marrow overproduction raises pressure inside the bones; assess by applying light to moderate fingertip pressure on the sternum. Assess joint range of motion and document swelling or pain.
Abdominal — the absolute rule: do not palpate the splenic area in any patient with a suspected hematologic problem. The normal adult spleen is not palpable; an enlarged one is tender and ruptures easily, which can lead to hemorrhage and death. The provider performs splenic and liver palpation.
GI bleeding as a hidden cause — chronic bleeding from an ulcer or intestinal polyps causes anemia, and the amount passed daily may be so small the patient is unaware. Ask about changes in bowel habits and blood in the stool, and obtain a specimen for occult blood testing.
Central nervous system — vitamin B₁₂ deficiency impairs nerve function, and severe chronic deficiency can cause permanent neurologic degeneration. Leukemias and lymphomas can involve or spread to the CNS. With any head trauma in a suspected bleeding disorder, expand to frequent neurologic checks.
Aging adaptation — nail beds: thickened or discolored nails make capillary refill hard to read, so use the lips, tongue, palpebral conjunctiva, palms, or soles instead.
Aging adaptation — hair distribution: progressive loss of body hair, especially on the extremities, is normal with aging, so a relatively even pattern of loss over a long period is not a perfusion finding and should not be used as one.
Aging adaptation — skin moisture: reduced turgor and dryness are normal in aging skin, so skin moisture is not a reliable indicator of a hematologic problem; investigate further to decide whether reduced turgor is aging or disease.
Aging adaptation — skin color: pigment changes are common with aging, so use laboratory results rather than skin color to monitor for anemia and jaundice.
The aging blood itself — decreased blood volume with lower plasma proteins (less dietary protein, less production by the aging liver); marrow produces fewer cells, so red cell counts, hemoglobin, and hematocrit are lower, and platelet counts are reduced after 60. Lymphocytes become less reactive to antigens, antibody levels are lower and slower, and the white count may not rise as high in response to infection as in a younger adult.
The peripheral blood smear — a drop of blood spread on a slide, read digitally or by microscope, giving rapid information on the sizes, shapes, and proportions of red cells, white cells, and platelets.
The reference values — RBC 4.2–5.4 × 10⁶/µL (female) and 4.7–6.1 (male): raised in chronic hypoxia such as COPD, severe dehydration, and polycythemia vera; lowered in anemia, cirrhosis, and hemorrhage. Hemoglobin 12–16 g/dL (female) and 14–18 (male), with critical values below 7 or above 21 g/dL.
Psychosocial — every patient brings their own coping mechanisms. Build rapport, learn what has worked for them before, and collaborate with the provider about a mental health referral when indicated.
Why
The instruction not to palpate the spleen is worth separating from the rest of the exam, because it inverts the usual logic of assessment. Normally a suspicious finding earns a closer look; here the closer look is the danger. An enlarged spleen is engorged, thin-walled, and under pressure, and the maneuver that would confirm it can rupture it — so the finding is confirmed by imaging and by the provider, not by your hands.
The nursing process for this chapter

Blood does three jobs — carries oxygen, defends, and seals leaks. The assessment is organized around those three, because one organ failing shows up as three apparently separate complaints: fatigue, infections, and bruising.

A — Assess
History
Age and sex (marrow function and immunity decline with age; women run lower counts and lose more with menstruation — teach them to report cycle changes with breathlessness, dizziness, or fatigue). Past cancer and whether radiation was used. Occupation, hobbies, and proximity to industry. COVID-19 vaccination and infection history, including VITT screening 4–42 days after a Janssen or AstraZeneca dose.
Drugs, diet, and social factors
Screen specifically for drugs causing marrow suppression (chemotherapy, methotrexate, allopurinol, ciprofloxacin), hemolytic anemia (cephalosporins, NSAIDs, penicillins, methyldopa), and thrombocytopenia (aspirin, heparin, furosemide, statins, phenytoin, and gold compounds even after stopping). Then diet over the past week, alcohol, and social determinants — income, support, and whether they live in a food desert.
Family and current problems
Ask by name about hemophilia, von Willebrand disease, thalassemia, sickle cell disease and trait, and factor deficiencies — plus conditions of excessive clotting. Then lymph node swelling; bruising or bleeding and whether it was spontaneous or after trauma; bleeding after dental work; pads or tampons used last cycle and whether clots were present, sized against a common object; frequent infections, fevers, weight loss, headaches, paresthesias.
Physical, gently
Assess carefully enough not to cause bruising or bleeding, and hold pressure after venipuncture until bleeding stops. Pallor at gums, conjunctivae, nail beds, and stretched palmar creases — not skin alone. Petechiae, purpura, ecchymoses; bleeding around every tube and line. Mouth: fissures, smooth or beefy-red tongue. All lymph node areas, documenting size, tenderness, and whether fixed or mobile. Respiratory effort after walking 20 steps in 10 seconds and whether they can say a 10-word sentence. Sternal tenderness with light fingertip pressure. Never palpate the splenic area.
Why
The drug history is the highest-yield part of this assessment because so much hematologic disease is iatrogenic. Everyday drugs appear on these lists, and gold compounds can cause thrombocytopenia after they have been stopped — so the question is what the patient has taken in the past month, not only what they take now.
D — Diagnose & prioritize
Read the CBC as a pattern first: one line down points one way, all three down is pancytopenia and points at the marrow. Then the reticulocyte count answers the next question — low hemoglobin with a high retic means cells are being lost or destroyed; with a low retic, production itself has failed.
1. Potential for infection related to leukopenia or impaired immunity — ranked first whenever white cells are low, because the usual signs of infection are absent.
2. Potential for bleeding related to thrombocytopenia, clotting factor deficiency, or anticoagulant and antiplatelet drugs.
3. Decreased gas exchange / activity intolerance related to reduced oxygen-carrying capacity.
4. Inadequate nutrition related to iron, B₁₂, folate, or protein deficiency, or to malabsorption.
5. Anxiety related to an uncertain diagnosis and pending marrow biopsy.
Why
The anemia is what the patient feels most and still ranks third. That inversion is the recurring lesson of this chapter: what the patient notices and what is most dangerous are different lists, and in marrow failure the silent low white count outranks the fatigue they came in complaining about.
P — Plan
Remains free of infection through the workup; reports any temperature of 100.4°F (38°C) immediately.
No bleeding event beyond minor gingival oozing; drugs that impair clotting stopped or substituted.
Completes ADLs with rest periods and without dyspnea at rest; heart rate settling at rest.
Reticulocyte count rises as evidence the marrow is responding, with hemoglobin and hematocrit following.
Bone marrow aspiration and biopsy completed without bleeding or infection at the site.
Patient states their replacement therapy and route — and why an oral supplement will not work if the defect is absorption.
Why
The reticulocyte outcome is listed ahead of hemoglobin because a red cell lives about 120 days, so hemoglobin barely moves in the first week of correct treatment while reticulocytes rise within days. Watching the retic count is how you know the therapy is working before the patient feels any different.
I — Implement
Independent
Meticulous hand hygiene and oral care with a soft toothbrush or sponge. Screen visitors for illness. No IM injections, rectal temperatures, suppositories, or enemas. Electric razor; pressure after every stick. Fall prevention. Cluster care and pace activity with rest. Assess each shift for occult bleeding — stool, urine, gums, neurologic status — and measure abdominal girth daily, since an increase can mean internal hemorrhage.
Dependent
Serial CBC with differential and reticulocyte count; iron studies, B₁₂, folate, bilirubin, coagulation studies, smear. Replacement by the correct route — oral iron on an empty stomach with vitamin C, expect dark stools; parenteral B₁₂ when intrinsic factor is missing. Discontinue offending drugs. Transfusion with two-nurse verification, an 18–20 gauge line, normal saline only, and the first 15 minutes at the bedside.
Procedure care
Marrow aspiration and biopsy: consent, usually the posterior iliac crest, warn of brief sharp pressure and pain. After: pressure dressing, back for 10–15 minutes, monitor for bleeding and infection, mild analgesia, no strenuous activity for 24 hours. Discharge teaching: bandage dry 24 hours, no tub baths, pools, or hot tubs for a week, and report bleeding, swelling, increasing redness, fever, or increasing pain.
Collaborative
Hematology; dietitian for iron, B₁₂, folate, and protein within the patient’s budget and culture; pharmacy for substitutions; social work for food insecurity and drug cost; mental health referral where indicated.
Why
The no-rectal-anything rule reads as arbitrary until the mechanism is attached: rectal mucosa tears easily, and a patient who cannot clot bleeds from that tear while a patient who cannot fight infection seeds bacteria through it. One rule, both risks — which is why it appears identically in leukemia, aplastic anemia, and chemotherapy care plans.
E — Evaluate
Counts are the objective measure — hemoglobin and platelets rising, white count recovering, and the reticulocyte count climbing. Clinically: no fever, no new petechiae or bruising, heart rate settling, and stairs manageable again. Nutritional deficiencies corrected and the patient can state their regimen.
Unmet looks like: a further fall in any cell line, a temperature of 100.4°F, bleeding that does not stop with pressure, a rising abdominal girth, or headache, vision change, or altered consciousness signalling intracranial bleeding.
Why
Evaluation here runs on a slower clock than in the cardiac chapters, and the retic count is what makes it readable at all — without it, a week of correct treatment and a week of wrong treatment look identical on the hemoglobin.

34 · Chapter 34
Clotting · immunity · cell regulation

Hematologic conditions

On Exam 2 · 20 questionsLab value interpretation (e.g., anemia types)·Sickle cell — patient education·Anemia: iron deficiency, pernicious, folic acid & vitamin B12 (Table 34.1)·Leukemia

On examIntro. Three failures, in order: too few red cells (anemia), too few or too many white cells (leukemia, lymphoma, myeloma, neutropenia), and clotting gone wrong (thrombocytopenia, DIC). The nursing priorities repeat: oxygen delivery, infection prevention, and bleeding precautions.

On exam
Connection Map How this chapter fits together
1 · Normal physiologyNormal red cells are flexible, live ~120 days and carry oxygen; clotting balances bleeding.
2 · What failsToo few cells (anemia), abnormal cells (sickle, leukemia), too many (polycythemia) or failed clotting.
3 · What the nurse seesSickle crisis pain, jaundice, infections, bleeding, clots, fatigue.
4 · What proves itCBC with MCV, reticulocytes, iron/B₁₂, hemoglobin electrophoresis, marrow biopsy.
5 · What targets itHydration/oxygen/opioids in sickle crisis; iron/B₁₂; transfusion; infection and bleeding precautions.
6 · If it worsensAcute chest syndrome, stroke, sepsis, transfusion reaction.
7 · Emergency triggerChest pain with fever in sickle cell, back pain/chills during transfusion, neutropenic fever.
Cause and effect
Low oxygen, dehydration or cold→HbS polymerizes→cells sickle and stick→vessels occlude→ischemic pain → hydrate, oxygenate, treat pain, keep warm
Iron vs B₁₂ deficiency
Iron: microcytic. B₁₂: macrocytic with neurologic changes.
Why the drug makes sense
Oral iron: calcium binds iron → take away from dairy, with vitamin C. Epoetin: more RBCs → thicker blood → clots when Hgb is too high. Drug cards →
